### Q57. JEE Main 2023 (08 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

In chromyl chloride, the number of d-electrons present on chromium is same as in (Given at no. of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ti</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>:</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>22</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>:</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>23</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Cr</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>:</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>24</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Mn</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>:</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>25</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Fe</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>:</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>26</mn></math>)

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>IV</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Mn</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>VII</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Fe</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>III</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ti</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>III</mi><mo>)</mo></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>Mn</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>VII</mi><mo>)</mo></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>In chromyl chloride (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CrO</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>), the oxidation state of chromium <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi>Cr</mi><mo>)</mo></math> is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>.</mo></math></p><p>So, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cr</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>VI</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><msup><mo>]</mo><mn>18</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>0</mn></msup></math></p><p>For the given elements the configuration is,<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>VII</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><msup><mo>]</mo><mn>18</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>0</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Fe</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>III</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><msup><mo>]</mo><mrow><mn>18</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></mrow></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>5</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ti</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>III</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><msup><mo>]</mo><mn>18</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>IV</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><msup><mo>]</mo><mn>18</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math></p><p>So among the given options <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>VII</mi><mo>)</mo></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cr</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>VI</mi><mo>)</mo></math> will have same number of d-electrons.</p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q58. JEE Main 2023 (06 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>In ammonium<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo></math>phosphomolybdate, the oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mo</mi></math> is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi><mi>_</mi></math></p>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **6**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>Phosphomolybdate is an anion containing both phosphorus and molybdenum, with the chemical formula <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mfenced><msub><mi>NH</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></mfenced><mn>3</mn></msub><msub><mi>PO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#183;</mo><mn>12</mn><msub><mi>MoO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>. </p><p>The ammonium ion has a total charge of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></math>, and the phosphate ion has a total charge of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn></math>. Therefore, the <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>12</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi>MoO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> units in the compound must have a total charge of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>.</p><p>Each <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>MoO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> unit contains one <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mo</mi></math> atom and three <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math> atoms. The total oxidation state of the three <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math> atoms is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>6</mn></math>, since each <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math> atom has an oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn></math>.</p><p>So, </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mo</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mspace linebreak="newline"/><mi>Mo</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math></p><p> </p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q59. JEE Main 2023 (01 Feb Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Given below are two statements : one is labelled as Assertion (A) and the other is labelled as Reason (R).
Assertion (A) : <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> in water is more stable than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>.
Reason (R) : Enthalpy of hydration for <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> is much less than that of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>.</p><p>In the light of the above statements, choose the correct answer from the options given below :
 </p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** Both (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A).
- **(B)** (A) is correct but (R) is not correct.
- **(C)** (A) is not correct but (R) is correct.
- **(D)** Both (A) and (R) are correct but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A).

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) Both (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A).**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#8594;</mo><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>+</mo><mi>Cu</mi></math>
The stability of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>)</mo></math> rather than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>)</mo></math>, is due to the much more negative <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mo>&#8710;</mo><mi>hyd</mi></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></math> of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>)</mo></math> than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>(</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>)</mo></math>, which more than compensates for the second ionisation enthalpy of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi></math>.</p><p>Hence, both (A) and (R) are correct and (R) is the correct explanation of (A).</p>

</details>

### Q60. JEE Main 2023 (31 Jan Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The correct order of basicity of oxides of vanadium is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>: In, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>, the oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtext>V</mtext></math> is as follows</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>6</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mfrac><mn>6</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn></math></p><p>The oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math> in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> is +3. The vanadium is present in its lowest oxidation state. The vanadium tends to donate the pair of electrons. Hence, it is basic oxide.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>: In <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>, the oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math> is as follows</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>10</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mfrac><mn>10</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>5</mn></math></p><p>The oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math> in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math> is +5. The Vanadium tends to donate and accept the pair of electrons. It is amphoteric oxide.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>: In <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>, the oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math> is as follows</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mfrac><mn>8</mn><mn>2</mn></mfrac><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>4</mn></math></p><p>The oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math> in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math> is +4. The Vanadium is present in its other low oxidation state. The Vanadium tends to donate and accept the pair of electrons. It is amphoteric oxide and less basic than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>, more basic than <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>.</p><p>So, the correct order of basicity of oxides of vanadium is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>&#62;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math>.</p>

</details>

### Q61. JEE Main 2023 (29 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>The set of correct statements is:</p><p>(i) Manganese exhibits <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>7</mn></math> oxidation state in its oxide.</p><p>(ii) Ruthenium and Osmium exhibit <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>8</mn></math> oxidation in their oxides.</p><p>(iii) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Sc</mi></math> shows <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn></math> oxidation state which is oxidizing in nature.</p><p>(iv) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cr</mi></math> shows oxidising nature in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math> oxidation state.</p>

- **(A)** (ii) and (iii)
- **(B)** (i), (ii) and (iv)
- **(C)** (i) and (iii)
- **(D)** (ii), (iii) and (iv)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) (i), (ii) and (iv)**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>(i) The oxidation state of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi></math> in oxide (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Mn</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi></math>.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>14</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>14</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>2</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>14</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>7</mn></math></p><p>(ii) The oxidation state of Ruthenium in oxide (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>RuO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>) is:</p><p>Consider <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ru</mi></math> as <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>x</mi></math>.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>8</mn></math></p><p>The oxidation state of Osmium in oxide (<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>OsO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>) is:</p><p>Consider <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Os</mi></math> as <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi></math>.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>(</mo><mo>-</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>-</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#8658;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">x</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>=</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>8</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math> </p><p>Hence, Ruthenium and Osmium has +7 oxidation state in its oxide.</p><p>(iii) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Sc</mi></math> has <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>ns</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>)</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math> electronic configuration.</p><p>It utilizes two electrons from its <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>ns</mi></math> sub-shell then its oxidation state = +2. When it utilizes both the electrons then its oxidation state = +3.</p><p>(iv) In the modern periodic table, chromium metal is present in group 6. It is a transition metal and belongs to the first transition series. The atomic number of chromium is 24. Its electronic configuration is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>5</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>4</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>1</mn></msup></math>. </p><p>It has five electrons in the 3d sub-shell and one electron in the 4s sub-shell. Thus, chromium metal has six valence electrons. Chromium can lose some or all of its valence electrons to form ions with different oxidation states. Thus, chromium shows the oxidation states of +1, +2, +3, +4, +5, and +6 respectively.
The most common oxidation states of chromium are +2, +3, and +6.</p><p>So, the statement (i), (ii) and (iv) are correct.</p>

</details>

### Q62. JEE Main 2023 (29 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `3`

A solution of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CrO</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math> in amyl alcohol has a....colour

**Options:**

- **(A)** Green
- **(B)** Orange-Red
- **(C)** Yellow
- **(D)** Blue

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) Blue**

**Detailed Solution:**

Chromium peroxide <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><msub><mi>CrO</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></mfenced></math> is a very unstable deep blue compound. It is soluble in water, but rapidly breaks down, and soluble as well as stable in solvents such as diethyl ether, ethyl acetate, amyl alcohol and its esters.

</details>

xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>NH</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mi>OH</mi></math></td>			<td> </td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>V</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ba</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Sr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Ca</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><msub><mi>NH</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><msub><mo>)</mo><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi>CO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> in presence of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>NH</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mi>OH</mi></math></td>			<td> </td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>VI</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td>None</td>			<td> </td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>So, the correct answer is <math ### Q64. JEE Main 2023 (24 Jan Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>Which one amongst the following are good oxidising agents?</p><p>(a) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sm</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></p><p>(b) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ce</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></p><p>(c) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ce</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></p><p>(d) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Tb</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></p><p>Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below :</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** C only
- **(B)** D only
- **(C)** A and B only
- **(D)** C and D only

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) C and D only**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>The common oxidation state of lanthanide is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></math>. The lanthanide ions having <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></math> oxidation state undergo oxidation readily, hence they act as reducing agents. The lanthanide ions having  <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math>oxidation state undergo reduction readily, hence they act as oxidising agents.</p><p>Hence, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ce</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>and</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Tb</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></msup></math> act as oxidising agent</p>

</details>

### Q66. JEE Main 2022 (29 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Which of the following <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>metal</mi></math> ion will give the lowest enthalpy of hydration <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">&#916;</mi><mi>hyd</mi></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi></mrow></mfenced></math> when dissolved in water?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Co</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>Hydration enthalpy (ꕔH<sub>Hyd</sub>) is the change in enthalpy when one mole of gaseous ion under a standard condition of 1 bar pressure dissolves in a sufficient amount of water to form an infinitely dilute solution (infinite dilution means a further addition of solute will not cause any heat change).</p><p>Hydration enthalpy is inversely related to the size of the ion, smaller the size of the ion greater is the hydration and hence greater is the hydration energy released.</p><p>Mn<sup>+2</sup> has the lowest hydration energy among the given ions as it has the largest size.</p><table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 500px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>Ion</strong></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi>&#916;H</mi><mrow><mi>Hyd</mi><mo>.</mo></mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">o</mi></msubsup><mo>(</mo><mi>kJ</mi><mo>/</mo><mi>mole</mi><mo>)</mo></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>1925</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>1862</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>1998</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Co</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>2079</mn></math></td>		</tr>	</tbody></table></blockquote>

</details>

### Q67. JEE Main 2022 (29 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

In following pairs, the one in which both transition metal ions are colourless is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></math>-block elements form coloured ions due to presence of unpaired electrons in them. since there are no unpaired electrons in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> ions, they are colourless.</p><p>No of unpaired <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>-</mo></msup></math></p><p> <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>1</mn></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>9</mn></msup></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>0</mn></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>10</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>T</mi><msup><mi>i</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>0</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>-</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>,</mo></math></p><p>No d-d transitions in ions with <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>0</mn></msup></math> &amp; <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>10</mn></msup></math> configuration. Therefore they are colourless.</p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q68. JEE Main 2022 (28 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Which of the following has least tendency to liberate <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> from mineral acids?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Zn</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 200px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;">Element <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></mfenced></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mo>&#8861;</mo></msup><mo>/</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>18</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>25</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>34</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Zn</mi></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>76</mn></math></td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>As we can see that Copper is least electropositive metal among the given options. So, it does not react with dilute sulphuric acid or other mineral acids, liberating hydrogen because copper is lower in electrochemical series than hydrogen. All other metals will react with mineral acids to liberate hydrogen gas.</p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q69. JEE Main 2022 (26 Jul Shift 2)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The spin-only magnetic moment value of the compound with strongest oxidizing ability among <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>MnF</mi><mn>4</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>MnF</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>MnF</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is_____ <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math> (nearest integer)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **5**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>The change from Mn<sup>3</sup><sup>+</sup> to Mn<sup>2</sup><sup>+</sup> results in the half-filled (d<sup>5</sup> ) configuration which has extra stability.So, MnF<sub>3</sub> has most oxidising tendency.</p><p>Hence, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>MnF</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> has the strongest oxidizing ability as it undergoes reduction readily.</p><p>Spin only magnetic moment is calculated using the following formula</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mfenced><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced></msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>4</mn><mfenced><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced></msqrt><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>24</mn></msqrt><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>89</mn><mo>=</mo><mn>5</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

---

### Q70. JEE Main 2022 (25 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Among <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Co</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> ions, one if used as a reagent cannot liberate <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> from dilute mineral acid solution, its spin-only magnetic moment in gaseous state is _____  B.M. (Nearest integer)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **5**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>Among the given species <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Co</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> cannot undergo oxidation, hence, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Co</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math> can&#39;t liberate <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>.</p><p>It has <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>6</mn></msup></math> configuration,</p><p>Number of unpaired electrons <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mfenced><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced></msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mspace linebreak="newline"/><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>4</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><mn>6</mn></msqrt><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>92</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mo>.&#160;</mo></math></p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q71. JEE Main 2022 (29 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

An acidified manganate solution undergoes disproportionation reaction. The spin-only magnetic moment value of the product having manganese in higher oxidation state is____B.M. (Nearest integer)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **0**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msubsup><mi>MnO</mi><mn>4</mn><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>-</mo></mrow></msubsup><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>&#10230;</mo><mn>2</mn><msubsup><mi>MnO</mi><mn>4</mn><mo>-</mo></msubsup><mo>+</mo><msub><mi>MnO</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></p><p>As we can see in this reaction oxidised product of reaction is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msubsup><mi>MnO</mi><mn>4</mn><mo>-</mo></msubsup></math> which has no unpaired electrons. So, magnetic moment will be zero.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Mn</mi><mo>=</mo></math> no. of unpaired electrons is &#39;<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>0</mn></math>&#39;</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p>

</details>

### Q72. JEE Main 2022 (28 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Dihydrogen reacts with <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>CuO</mi></math> to give

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><msub><mfenced><mi>OH</mi></mfenced><mn>2</mn></msub></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mfenced></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cu</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuH</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mfenced></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>CuO</mi><mover><mo>&#8594;</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mover><mi>Cu</mi><mo>+</mo><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></p><p>Copper oxide undergoes reduction to form elemental copper and water. This is a redox reaction. Since oxygen is removed from the <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>CuO</mi></math> it is undergoing reduction. And hydrogen is getting oxidised.</p>

</details>

### Q74. JEE Main 2022 (27 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>The number of statement(s) correct from the following for Copper is/are</p><p>(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced></math> complexes are always paramagnetic</p><p>(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced></math> complexes are generally colourless</p><p>(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced></math> is easily oxidized</p><p>(D) In Fehling solution, the active reagent has <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></mfenced></math></p>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **3**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi><mo>)</mo></math> have fulfilled d-subshell and does not have any unpaired electrons so, its complexes will be colourless mostly. and it will be diamagnetic. But <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mo>(</mo><mi>II</mi><mo>)</mo></math> has one unpaired electron, so, it will be paramagnetic. </p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">A</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">C</mi></math> are correct and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">D</mi></math> is incorrect because Fehling solution has <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mfenced><mi>II</mi></mfenced></math></p>

</details>

### Q75. JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The spin-only magnetic moment value of the most basic oxide of vanadium among <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>&#39; <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math> is____B.M. (Nearest integer)

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **3**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> is the most basic oxide among the given oxides.</p><p>In <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>, Vanadium is in <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></math> oxidation state</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#8658;</mo><mfenced close="]" open="["><mi>Ar</mi></mfenced><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>4</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi><mn>0</mn></msup></math></p><p>So, <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn></math> unpaired electrons are there,</p><p>Its magnetic moment is equal to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mfenced><mrow><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced></msqrt><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>BM</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>2</mn><mfenced><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></mrow></mfenced></msqrt><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>BM</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>83</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>BM</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#8771;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>B</mi><mi>M</mi></math></p><p>(<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>=</mo></math> number of unpaired electrons)</p>

</details>

### Q76. JEE Main 2022 (26 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Choose the correct stability order of group <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>13</mn></math> elements in their <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></math> oxidation state.

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Ga</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>In</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Tl</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Tl</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>In</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Ga</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>In</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Tl</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Ga</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ga</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>In</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Tl</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Al</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Ga</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>In</mi><mo>&#60;</mo><mi>Tl</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<blockquote><p>On moving down the group, the stability of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math>oxidation state decreases while <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math>oxidation state increases due to inert pair effect.</p></blockquote>

</details>

### Q77. JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `3`

The metal ion (in gaseous state) with lowest spin-only magnetic moment value is

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ni</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ni</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 500px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;"> </td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>Ion</strong></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>Electronic configuration</strong></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><strong>No. of unpaired electron</strong></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;">1</td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>4</mn></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;">2</td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;">3</td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>6</mn></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>4</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td style="text-align: center;">4</td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ni</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mn>8</mn></msup></math></td>			<td style="text-align: center;"><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>2</mn></math></td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>Magnetic moment(spin only) is calculated by the formula <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo></msqrt><mi>BM</mi></math>.</p><p>Magnetic moment (spin only)is directly proportional to the number of unpaired electrons.</p><p>Hence, less is the number of unpaired electrons less is the magnetic moment (spin only).</p><p>As Ni<sup>2+ </sup>has the least number of unpaired electrons it has the least magnetic moment (spin only) among the given options.</p>

</details>

### Q78. JEE Main 2022 (25 Jun Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Among the following, which is the strongest oxidizing agent?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>The substance which will have the highest value of reduction potential will be the strongest oxidising agent. The values of reduction potential are as follow</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtable columnalign="right left right left right left right left right left right left" columnspacing="0em 2em 0em 2em 0em 2em 0em 2em 0em 2em 0em" rowspacing="3pt"><mtr><mtd><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mrow><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>/</mo><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mrow><mn>0</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.57</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mrow><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>/</mo><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mrow><mn>0</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.37</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mrow><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>/</mo><msup><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mrow><mn>0</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mo>+</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.77</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msubsup><mi mathvariant="normal">E</mi><mrow><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>/</mo><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></mrow><mn>0</mn></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mo>&#8722;</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>.41</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi></mtd></mtr></mtable></math></p><p>As we can see <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msup></math> has the highest value of standard reduction potential, so it will be the strongest oxidising agent.</p>

</details>

### Q79. JEE Main 2021 (01 Sep Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

### Q80. JEE Main 2021 (27 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The nature of oxides <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>CrO</mi></math> is indexed as <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>'</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>'</mo></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>'</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>'</mo></math> type respectively. The correct set of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi></math> is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>=</mo></math>acidic <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>=</mo></math>acidic
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>=</mo></math>amphoteric
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>=</mo></math>amphoteric <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">X</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">Y</mi><mo>=</mo></math>basic**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>Generally oxides of metals ae basic and oxides of non-metal are acidic.</p><table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 500px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td>Oxide</td>			<td>Nature</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(i) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></td>			<td>Basic</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(ii) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>CrO</mi></math></td>			<td>Basic</td>		</tr>	</tbody></table>

</details>

### Q81. JEE Main 2021 (26 Aug Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

Which one of the following when dissolved in water gives coloured solution in nitrogen atmosphere?

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cu</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>ZnCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>AgCl</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> dissolve in water &amp; give blue coloured solutions</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi>nH</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#8594;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>)</mo></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>AgCl</mi></math> not soluble in water.
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cu</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi>Cl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is not soluble in water.
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>ZnCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is soluble but colourless.</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>ZnCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>+</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi>nH</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>&#8594;</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>(</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>aq</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><mo>)</mo><mo>&#160;</mo></math>colourless</p>

</details>

### Q82. JEE Main 2021 (25 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

<p>The correct order of following <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>3</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></math> metal oxides, according to their oxidation numbers is :</p><p>(a) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CrO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></p><p>(b) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Fe</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></p><p>(c) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>MnO</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></p><p>(d) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math></p><p>(e) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>Cu</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>)</mo><mo>&#62;</mo><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>)</mo></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>(a) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mover><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></mrow></mover><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></p><p>(b) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mover><mi>Fe</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math></p><p>(c) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mover><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></mover><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></p><p>(d) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mover><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>5</mn></mrow></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math></p><p>(e) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mover><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mover><mn>2</mn></msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi></math></p><p>So order of oxidation state</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>&#62;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi></math></p>

</details>

### Q83. JEE Main 2021 (22 Jul Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The set having ions which are coloured and paramagnetic both is -

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Zn</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mrow><mn>5</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Ti</mi><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Ni</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Mn</mi><mrow><mn>7</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Hg</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>,</mo><mo>&#160;</mo><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mo>+</mo></msup></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cu</mi><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>:</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></mrow><mn>9</mn></msup><mn>4</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>0</mn></msup></math>
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Cr</mi><mrow><mn>3</mn><mo>+</mo></mrow></msup><mo>:</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></mrow><mn>3</mn></msup><mn>4</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>0</mn></msup></math>
<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi>Sc</mi><mo>+</mo></msup><mo>:</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></mrow><mn>1</mn></msup><mn>4</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>1</mn></msup></math></p><p>All are coloured and paramagnetic due to presence of unpaired electrons.</p>

</details>

### Q84. JEE Main 2021 (18 Mar Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

<p>Match List<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></math> with List<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mi>II</mi></math></p><table border="1" cellpadding="1" cellspacing="1" style="width: 500px;">	<tbody>		<tr>			<td> </td>			<td>List<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">I</mi></math> (process)</td>			<td> </td>			<td>List<math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>-</mo><mi>II</mi></math> (catalyst)</td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(a)</td>			<td>Deacon&#39;s process</td>			<td>(i)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>ZSM</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>5</mn></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(b)</td>			<td>Contact process</td>			<td>(ii)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(c)</td>			<td>Cracking of hydrocarbons</td>			<td>(iii)</td>			<td> <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math></td>		</tr>		<tr>			<td>(d)</td>			<td>Hydrogenation of vegetable oils</td>			<td>(iv)</td>			<td><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math></td>		</tr>	</tbody></table><p>Choose the most appropriate answer from the options given below-</p>

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iv</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iii</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iv</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iv</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iv</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iii</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi mathvariant="normal">a</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>ii</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">b</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iv</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">c</mi><mo>-</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi><mo>-</mo><mi>iii</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

In manufacture of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi>SO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math> (contact process), <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">V</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">O</mi><mn>5</mn></msub></math> is used as a catalyst. <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math> catalysts enables the hydrogenation of fats. <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>CuCl</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></math> is used as catalyst in Deacon&#39;s process. <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>ZSM</mi><mo>-</mo><mn>5</mn></math> used as catalyst in cracking of hydrocarbons.

</details>

### Q85. JEE Main 2021 (17 Mar Shift 2)

*Type:* `numerical` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

In the ground state of atomic <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Fe</mi><mo>(</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">Z</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>26</mn><mo>)</mo></math>, the spin-only magnetic moment is <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>&#215;</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mi>BM</mi></math>. (Round off to the Nearest Integer).
[ Given <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>:</mo><msqrt><mn>3</mn></msqrt><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>73</mn><mo>,</mo><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>41</mn><mo>]</mo></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Value:** **49**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Fe</mi><mo>&#8594;</mo><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>4</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></mrow><mn>6</mn></msup></math> <img src="https://cdn-question-pool.getmarks.app/pyq/jee_main/ae4a1338-7d6d-4210-8759-e4bdc5872c1b-image.png" style="width: 224px; height: 62px;" /></p><p>Number of unpaired <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msup><mi mathvariant="normal">e</mi><mo>-</mo></msup><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>4</mn><mo>(</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>)</mo></msqrt><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi><mo>.</mo></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mn>24</mn></msqrt><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>4</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>89</mn><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>&#956;</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>48</mn><mo>.</mo><mn>9</mn><mo>&#215;</mo><msup><mn>10</mn><mrow><mo>-</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msup><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">B</mi><mo>.</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">M</mi></math></p><p>Nearest integer value will be <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>49</mn><mo>.</mo></math></p>

</details>

### Q86. JEE Main 2021 (17 Mar Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `2`

The common positive oxidation states for an element with atomic number <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mn>24</mn><mo>,</mo></math> are:

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>&#160;</mo></math>and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>Cr</mo><mo>(</mo><mi>Z</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>24</mn><mo>)</mo></math></p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>[</mo><mi>Ar</mi><mo>]</mo><mn>4</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">s</mi></mrow><mn>1</mn></msup><mn>3</mn><msup><mrow><mo>&#160;</mo><mi mathvariant="normal">d</mi></mrow><mn>5</mn></msup><mi>Cr</mi></math> shows common oxidation states starting from <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>2</mn></math> to <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn></math>.</p>

</details>

### Q87. JEE Main 2021 (16 Mar Shift 1)

Which of the following is Lindlar catalyst?

**Options:**

- **(A)** Zinc chloride and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>HCl</mi></math>
- **(B)** Cold dilute solution of <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>KMnO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></math>
- **(C)** Sodium and Liquid <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><msub><mi>NH</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></math>
- **(D)** Partially deactivated palladised charcoal

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

Partially deactivated palladised charcoal <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mfenced><mrow><msub><mi mathvariant="normal">H</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>/</mo><mi>Pd</mi><mo>/</mo><msub><mi>CaCO</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>&#160;</mo><mi>or</mi><mo>&#160;</mo><msub><mi>BaSO</mi><mn>4</mn></msub></mrow></mfenced></math> is Lindlar catalyst.

</details>

### Q88. JEE Main 2021 (25 Feb Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Difficulty Level:* `1`

The major components of German Silver are :

**Options:**

- **(A)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Zn</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Ni</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math>
- **(B)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Zn</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math>
- **(C)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ge</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Cu</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math>
- **(D)** <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Zn</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ag</mi></math>

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Cu</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>Zn</mi></math> and <math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mi>Ni</mi></math>**

**Detailed Solution:**

<p>German silver is silvery-white in appearance. Hence, it was named German silver. But nowadays, if an alloy does not contain silver, then it cannot be named as silver. German silver is also called Nickel silver.</p><p>Major components of German silver are:</p><p><math xmlns="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><mtable><mtr><mtd><mi>Cu</mi><mo>,</mo></mtd><mtd><mi>Zn</mi><mo>,</mo></mtd><mtd><mi>Ni</mi></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mfenced><mrow><mn>50</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></mfenced></mtd><mtd><mfenced><mrow><mn>30</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></mfenced></mtd><mtd><mfenced><mrow><mn>20</mn><mo>%</mo></mrow></mfenced></mtd></mtr></mtable></math></p>

</details>
